Tight Lower Bounds and Optimal Algorithms for Stochastic Nonconvex Optimization with Heavy-Tailed Noise
Adrien Fradin, Abdurakhmon Sadiev, Laurent Condat, Peter Richtárik x

Abstract
We study stochastic nonconvex optimization under heavy-tailed noise. In this setting, the stochastic gradients only have bounded p p –th central moment ( p p –BCM) for some p ∈ ( 1 , 2 ] p\in\mathopen{(}1\mathclose{},2\mathclose{]} . Building on the foundational work of 4 in stochastic optimization, we establish tight sample complexity lower bounds for all first-order methods under relaxed mean-squared smoothness ( q q -WAS) and δ \delta -similarity ( ( q , δ ) (q,\delta) -S) assumptions, allowing any exponent q ∈ [ 1 , 2 ] q\in[1,2] instead of the standard q = 2 q=2 . These results substantially broaden the scope of existing lower bounds. To complement them, we show that Normalized Stochastic Gradient Descent with Momentum Variance Reduction ( NSGD-MVR ), a known algorithm, matches these bounds in expectation. Beyond expectation guarantees, we introduce a new algorithm, Double-Clipped NSGD-MVR , which allows the derivation of high-probability convergence rates under weaker assumptions than previous works. Finally, for second-order methods with stochastic Hessians satisfying bounded q q -th central moment assumptions for some exponent q ∈ [ 1 , 2 ] q\in[1,2] (allowing q ≠ p q\neq p ), we establish sharper lower bounds than previous works while improving over 65 (where only p = q p=q is considered) and yielding stronger convergence exponents. Together, these results provide a nearly complete complexity characterization of stochastic nonconvex optimization in heavy-tailed regimes.



1
Introduction

We consider the stochastic optimization problem

\min_{x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}F(x),\qquad F(x):={\mathbb{E}}_{\xi\sim\mathcal{D}}\left[f(x,\xi)\right],

where
d\geq 1
is the dimension,
F\colon\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}
is a smooth possibly nonconvex objective function, and
\xi
is a random variable drawn from an unknown distribution
\mathcal{D}
. In the nonconvex setting, our goal is to find an
\varepsilon
–stationary point
(
57
)
; that is, a (random) vector
\bar{x}
such that
\mathbb{E}[\left\|\nabla F(\bar{x})\right\|]\leq\varepsilon
. Problem of the form (
1
) are pervasive in machine learning where
f\left(x,\xi\right)
denotes the loss of a model with weights
x
on a data sample
\xi\sim\mathcal{D}
, and
\mathcal{D}
is the distribution of the training samples from the dataset
(
12
)
. While Gradient Descent (
GD
) is known to achieve the optimal rate
\mathcal{O}(\varepsilon^{-2})
for finding an
\varepsilon
–stationary point
(
13
)
, it requires access to exact gradients
\nabla F(\cdot)
which is infeasible in practice. Therefore, reliance on noisy gradients has become a gold standard approach, giving rise to stochastic gradient methods like Stochastic Gradient Descent (
SGD
)
(
62
)
. Under the standard
\sigma_{1}^{2}
–bounded variance assumption,
SGD
is provably optimal
(
27
)
, matching the lower bound
\Omega(\nicefrac{{L_{1}\Delta}}{{\varepsilon^{2}}}+\nicefrac{{L_{1}\Delta\sigma_{1}^{2}}}{{\varepsilon^{4}}})

(
4
)
for
L_{1}
–smooth functions with
\smash{\Delta:=F(x^{0})-F^{\inf}}
.

However, empirical observations in modern machine learning, e.g., image classification
(
69
;
70
;
11
)
, large language models
(
81
;
1
)
and reinforcement learning
(
26
)
, have shed light on the importance of
heavy-tailed
noise as a more realistic setting than the standard bounded variance assumption. That is, noisy gradients only have bounded
p
–th central moment,
p\in\mathopen{(}1\mathclose{},2\mathclose{]}
. Such a relaxed moment assumption introduces challenges on both the algorithmic and theoretical sides, as
SGD
may fail to converge when
p<2
. In the heavy-tailed regime,
81
establish a lower bound of
\Omega(T^{-\nicefrac{{(p-1)}}{{(3p-2)}}})
on the convergence rate of first-order methods, where
T
denotes the number of iterations. Yet, their analysis is confined to the standard stochastic setting, where algorithms access smooth (potentially nonconvex) functions via unbiased stochastic gradients. Crucially, they did not address more structured assumptions on the gradient noise such as
mean-squared smoothness
or
\delta
–similarity
, which play a central to the design and analysis of
variance-reduced
methods, but they remain largely unexplored in the heavy-tailed regime.

In this work, we aim to:
(a)

develop lower complexity bounds for first-order variance-reduced methods in the heavy-tailed regime
, thereby closing an important open question from
48
, while advancing beyond the results of
72
;
(b)

extend existing high-probability analysis to these new assumptions
, and finally, for second-order methods;
(c)

characterize how the tail indices
p
(of gradients) and
q
(of Hessians) jointly affect convergence in the heavy-tailed regime
.

Beyond the development of lower bounds, a natural question arises:

Does there exist algorithm(s) which provably match (in expectation) the lower bounds developed in
(a)
?

Here, we answer this question affirmatively by revisiting a classical technique: gradient normalization. Remarkably, our results show that this simple method remains effective even in the heavy-tailed regime.

for the case

.

We revisit the analysis of Normalized
SGD
(
NSGD
) with Momentum Variance Reduction (
MVR
) in the heavy-tailed regime. Our contributions are:

\bullet

Tight lower bounds.
We obtain novel and tight lower bounds on the sample complexity of any first-order methods in the
p
–BCM noise model, under
relaxed
mean-squared smoothness and
\delta
-similarity assumptions, allowing any exponent
q\in\mathopen{[}1\mathclose{},2\mathclose{]}
instead of
q=2
. Our bound,
\smash{\Omega(\varepsilon^{-\nicefrac{{(p(2q+1)-2q)}}{{q(p-1)}}})}
, recovers the known
\smash{\Omega(\varepsilon^{-3})}
when
p=q=2
and achieves an improvement over the minimax complexity for first-order methods
(
81
;
72
;
34
)
, thereby demonstrating the effectiveness of variance reduction in the heavy-tailed regime. Moreover, our result
continuously interpolates
between variance-reduced and non-variance-reduced regimes, as in the limit
q\to 1
we recover the known
\Omega(\varepsilon^{-\nicefrac{{(3p-2)}}{{(p-1)}}})
complexity.

Our proofs are based on a generalization of
4
to the
p
–BCM noise assumption, which resolves an open question from
48
.

\bullet

Optimal first-order methods.
Revisiting
NSGD-MVR
, we show that, with a suitable choice of parameters, the method matches the lower bounds (up to constant factors) with respect to all parameters, thereby establishing the optimality of our analysis and the robustness of gradient normalization in the context of heavy-tailed noises. We further provide convergence rates with
unknown
tail indices
p
and
q
, recovering the best known rate of
\mathcal{O}(\varepsilon^{-\nicefrac{{2p}}{{(p-1)}}})

(
50
)
.

\bullet

High-probability upper bounds.
We propose a clipped variant of
NSGD-MVR
leading to a
new
algorithm: Double-Clipped
NSGD-MVR
, which provably achieves high-probability convergence rates under weaker assumptions than previous works
(
48
)
.

\bullet

Extension to second-order methods.
Lastly, for second-order methods using stochastic Hessians with bounded
q
-th central moment, for some exponent
q\in\mathopen{[}1\mathclose{},2\mathclose{]}
, we establish sharper lower bounds than previous works
(
5
;
65
)
, extend previous analysis to the more general setting where
p\neq q
(unlike
65
), and derive upper bounds with stronger convergence exponents, thereby offering a more complete characterization of the limits of stochastic second-order optimization under heavy-tailed noise. Overall, our results reveal a striking parallel in the rates of variance-reduced methods and second-order optimization.

Table

1
.

1.2
Related Works

Lower bound in the heavy-tailed regime:
In nonconvex optimization,
81
;
50

establish a lower bound of
\smash{\Omega\big(\frac{L_{1}\Delta}{\varepsilon^{2}}+\frac{L_{1}\Delta}{\varepsilon^{2}}\left(\frac{\sigma_{1}}{\varepsilon}\right)^{\nicefrac{{p}}{{(p-1)}}}\big)}
under
p
–BCM noise and standard
L_{1}
–smoothness, recovering the well-known
\Omega(\varepsilon^{-4})
lower bound when
p=2
.
Other line of works focused on algorithm-dependent lower bounds:
NSGD

(
78
)
and recently
SGD

(
25
)
, highlighting the ineffectiveness of
SGD
under heavy-tailed noise with only bounded
p
-th (non-central) moments. Beyond first-order methods,
65
obtain near-optimal lower bounds for all second-order methods, extending the work of
5
to
p
–BCM noise.

Convergence under heavy-tailed noise:
A substantial body of work investigates upper bounds for stochastic optimization with heavy-tailed noise, both in expectation and high probability. For smooth and nonconvex objectives, clipped or normalized variants of
SGD
achieve the rate
\mathcal{O}(\varepsilon^{-\nicefrac{{(3p-2)}}{{(p-1)}}})
up to logarithmic factors
(
81
;
18
;
48
;
64
)
. Among these,
81
obtained the sharpest in-expectation guarantees, later shown to be tight by
34
via
minibatch-NSGD
, while
58
established improved high-probability guarantees, avoiding extra
\mathcal{O}(\log T)
factors. Later works extended known high-probability analyses to settings with higher-order smoothness
(
65
)
and to more general nonlinear
SGD
-type methods
(
9
;
10
)
. In parallel,
72
studied
clip-SGD
under stronger smoothness assumptions, obtaining a rate of
\mathcal{O}(\varepsilon^{-\nicefrac{{(2p-1)}}{{(p-1)}}})
, while
50
derived in-expectation bounds for
NSGD-Mom
under a general
(\sigma_{0},\sigma_{1})
–affine
p
–BCM model, albeit without matching lower bounds.

Gradient clipping and normalization:
Gradient clipping and normalization are two closely related techniques that have become central in modern optimization. Gradient clipping, originally popularized to stabilize training across various machine learning applications
(
59
;
67
)
, has been analyzed extensively, providing robustness under relaxed moment assumptions
(
60
;
37
)
, high-order smoothness
(
65
)
, and enabling high-probability convergence guarantees with only logarithmic dependence on the failure probability. These results hold in both convex
(
54
;
28
;
20
;
30
;
49
;
29
;
61
;
8
;
7
)
and nonconvex settings
(
81
;
18
;
64
;
58
;
48
;
65
)
. Importantly, high-probability guarantees are valuable both theoretically and practically, as they capture the behavior of individual runs rather than merely characterizing the average-case performance.

Normalized gradient methods date back to Nesterov’s pioneering work
(
55
)
and were later extended to the smooth and stochastic regimes
(
40
;
31
;
45
;
53
)
. In deep learning, normalization addresses exploding and vanishing gradients
(
79
;
80
)
, though rigorous nonconvex guarantees were first obtained by
17
, showing how Polyak’s momentum ensures convergence without large batches. Later works explored
NSGD
’s strengths and limits, including saddle-point escape
(
44
)
, lower bounds
(
78
)
and parameter-agnostic convergence
(
35
)
. Recently, extensions to heavy-tailed noise have been investigated
(
18
;
48
;
72
)
, though typically under stronger smoothness assumptions.

Despite strong empirical performance and complementarity with clipping, the theory of gradient normalization under heavy-tailed noise remains incomplete, motivating our contributions
.

Variance reduction for stochastic optimization:
Variance reduction originated as a tool to accelerate convergence in convex finite-sum optimization, with seminal works such as
63
;
38
;
68
;
51
;
21
introducing various algorithms, e.g.,
SAG
,
SVRG
,
SAGA
. Building on these foundations and the key advances of
2
, a sequence of works

(
33
;
42
;
82
;
71
;
41
)
developed algorithms attaining near-optimal or optimal rates under various regimes. In nonconvex optimization, variance reduction can also improve convergence: in the general stochastic setting (
1
), several works
(
24
;
19
;
75
;
47
;
46
;
39
)
established an
\mathcal{O}(\varepsilon^{-3})
convergence rate in expectation, which improves upon the classical
\Theta(\varepsilon^{-4})
of
SGD
and matches the minimax lower bound
\Omega(\varepsilon^{-3})
under mean-squared smoothness
(
4
)
. More recently, there has been growing interest in extending variance reduction techniques to the heavy-tailed regime
(
48
;
72
;
32
)
, where normalization and clipping have emerged as effective mechanisms to guarantee robustness under weaker moment assumptions.

In this work, we characterize the fundamental limits of first-order “variance-reduced” methods in the heavy-tailed regime and provide optimal methods which attain these limits
.

2
Notation and Assumptions

We review here the basic notation and assumptions needed in this paper (see
Appendix

A
for more details).

Notations.
For integer
n>0
,
[n]:=\left\{1,2,\ldots,n\right\}
. We let
d\geq 1
be the dimension,
\left\langle\cdot,\cdot\right\rangle
the standard dot product on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
,
\left\|\cdot\right\|
the
\ell^{2}
–norm and
\left\|\cdot\right\|_{\text{op}}
the canonical spectral/operator norm. Here,
\nabla f(\cdot,\cdot)
and
\nabla^{2}f(\cdot,\cdot)
denote the stochastic gradient and Hessian oracles;
a\wedge b:=\min\left\{a,b\right\}
and
a\vee b:=\max\left\{a,b\right\}
. We use the standard
\mathcal{O}(\cdot)
and
\Omega(\cdot)
for complexity notation. To avoid confusion, we use subscript
1
for gradient parameters (e.g.,
L_{1}
,
\sigma_{1}
) and
2
for Hessian ones.

In this work, we make the following assumptions.

Assumption 2.1

(Lower Boundedness)
.

The objective
F
is lower bounded:
F^{\inf}:=\inf_{x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}F(x)>-\infty
.

We then let
\Delta:=F(x^{0})-F^{\inf}
be the initial suboptimality where
x^{0}
is the starting point.

Assumption 2.2

(
L_{1}
–Lipschitz Gradients)
.

The objective
F
is differentiable over
\mathbb{R}^{d}
and its gradient is
L_{1}
–Lipschitz for some
L_{1}\geq 0
, i.e., for all
x,y\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
,

\left\|\nabla F(x)-\nabla F(y)\right\|\leq L_{1}\left\|x-y\right\|.

Assumption 2.3

(
p
–BCM for Gradients)
.

We have access to unbiased stochastic gradients
\nabla f(x,\xi)
, which have
p
-bounded central moment for some
p\in\mathopen{(}1\mathclose{},2\mathclose{]}
,
i.e., there exists
\sigma_{1}>0
such that for all
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
,

{\mathbb{E}}_{\xi\sim\mathcal{D}}\left[\nabla f(x,\xi)\right]=\nabla F(x)\quad\text{and}\quad{\mathbb{E}}_{\xi\sim\mathcal{D}}\left[\left\|\nabla f(x,\xi)-\nabla F(x)\right\|^{p}\right]\leq\sigma_{1}^{p}.

Assumption 2.4

(
L_{2}
–Lipschitz Hessians)
.

The objective
F
is twice continuously differentiable over
\mathbb{R}^{d}

and we have access to stochastic Hessian-vector products
\nabla^{2}f(x,\xi)\cdot v
for any
v\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
. Moreover, the Hessian of
F
is
L_{2}
–Lipschitz, i.e., for all
x,y\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
,

\left\|\nabla^{2}F(x)-\nabla^{2}F(y)\right\|_{\text{op}}\leq L_{2}\left\|x-y\right\|.

Assumption 2.5

(
q
–BCM for Hessians)
.

The stochastic Hessians
\nabla^{2}f(x,\xi)
are unbiased and have
q
-bounded central moment for some
q\in\mathopen{[}1\mathclose{},2\mathclose{]}
,
i.e., there exists
\sigma_{2}>0
such that for all
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}

{\mathbb{E}}_{\xi\sim\mathcal{D}}\left[\nabla^{2}f(x,\xi)\right]=\nabla^{2}F(x),\quad\text{and}\quad{\mathbb{E}}_{\xi\sim\mathcal{D}}\left[\left\|\nabla^{2}f(x,\xi)-\nabla^{2}F(x)\right\|_{\text{op}}^{q}\right]\leq\sigma_{2}^{q}.

Assumption 2.6

(
q
-Weak Average Smoothness
(
32
)
)
.

For some
q\in[1;2]
, there exists a finite constant
\bar{L}\geq 0
such that, for all
x,y\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
we have

{\mathbb{E}}_{\xi\sim\mathcal{D}}\left[\left\|\nabla f(x,\xi)-\nabla f(y,\xi)\right\|^{q}\right]\leq\bar{L}^{q}\left\|x-y\right\|^{q}.

The next assumption has been introduced in
5
in the special case
q=2
.

Assumption 2.7

(
(q,\delta)
–Similarity)
.

For some exponent
q\in[1;2]
, there exists a finite constant
\delta\geq 0
such that, for all
x,y\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
we have

{\mathbb{E}}_{\xi\sim\mathcal{D}}\left[\left\|\left[\nabla f(x,\xi)-\nabla f(y,\xi)\right]-\left[\nabla F(x)-\nabla F(y)\right]\right\|^{q}\right]\leq\delta^{q}\left\|x-y\right\|^{q}.

3
Lower Complexity Bounds

To establish our lower bounds, we rely on a technique developed in
13
, where a “worst-case” nonconvex function
F_{T}\colon\mathbb{R}^{T}\to\mathbb{R}
is introduced, which is hard to optimize for any zero-respecting algorithm. Our main novelty here is
Lemma

C.1
, an important result which lies at the core of all our lower bounds; it is a generalization of
4
to the
p
–BCM assumption, yielding an improvement from
\Omega(\nicefrac{{\sigma_{1}^{2}}}{{\varepsilon^{2}}})
to
\Omega((\nicefrac{{\sigma_{1}}}{{\varepsilon}})^{\nicefrac{{p}}{{(p-1)}}})
. This lemma handles a critical case in all
Theorems

3.1
,
3.2
and
3.3
when the parameters force the dimension
T
of the “hard instance”
F_{T}
to be too small. More details along with the main motivations behind this generalization are provided in
Remark

C.4
. We recall all the necessary definitions (function class, oracle class…) and the “hard instance”
F_{T}
from
13
in
Appendix

B
. The proofs of the following three lower bounds can be found in
Sections

C.3
,
C.4
and
C.5
.

3.1
Under
q
-Weak Average Smoothness

We first establish a lower bound on the oracle complexity of any zero-respecting algorithm under
2.6
, an extension of the mean-squared smoothness (MSS) assumption. This result generalizes one of the core contributions of
4
, who derived the optimal lower bound in the
p=q=2
setting.

Theorem 3.1
.

Given
\Delta,\bar{L}>0
,
\sigma_{1}\geq 0
and
0<\varepsilon\leq c_{1}\sqrt{\bar{L}\Delta}
for some universal constant
c_{1}>0
. Then, for any algorithm
A\in\mathcal{A}_{\texttt{zr}}
, there exists a function
f\in\mathcal{F}\left(\Delta\right)
, an oracle and a distribution
(O,\mathcal{D})\in\mathcal{O}\left(f,\bar{L}^{q},\sigma_{1}^{p}\right)
such that

\mathfrak{m}^{\texttt{zr}}_{\varepsilon}\left(K,\bar{L},\Delta,\sigma_{1}^{p}\right)\geq\Omega(1)\cdot\left(\left(\frac{\sigma_{1}}{\varepsilon}\right)^{\frac{p}{p-1}}+\frac{\bar{L}\Delta}{\varepsilon^{2}}+\frac{\bar{L}\Delta}{\varepsilon^{2}}\left(\frac{\sigma_{1}}{\varepsilon}\right)^{\frac{p}{q(p-1)}}\right).

In the special case
p=q=2
, our result recovers the optimal complexity lower bound
\Omega(\nicefrac{{\sigma_{1}^{2}}}{{\varepsilon^{2}}}+\nicefrac{{\bar{L}\Delta}}{{\varepsilon^{2}}}+\nicefrac{{\bar{L}\Delta\sigma_{1}}}{{\varepsilon^{3}}})
derived by
4
. We further establish the tightness of this bound in
Theorem

4.1
, where we prove a matching upper bound.

3.2
Under
(q,\delta)
–Similarity

The previous bound captures limits of first-order methods under
2.6
, but this assumption couples smoothness and noise together into
\bar{L}
. Assuming separately
(q,\delta)
–similarity with
L_{1}
-smoothness decouples these effects, yielding sharper lower bounds.

Theorem 3.2
.

Given
\Delta,L_{1},\delta>0
,
\sigma_{1}\geq 0
and
0<\varepsilon\leq c_{1}\sqrt{L_{1}\Delta}
for some universal constant
c_{1}>0
. Then, for any algorithm
A\in\mathcal{A}_{\texttt{zr}}
, there exists a function
f\in\mathcal{F}\left(\Delta,L_{1}\right)
, an oracle and a distribution
(O,\mathcal{D})\in\mathcal{O}\left(f,\delta^{q},\sigma_{1}^{p}\right)
such that

\displaystyle\mathfrak{m}^{\texttt{zr}}_{\varepsilon}\left(K,L_{1},\Delta,\delta,\sigma_{1}^{p}\right)

\displaystyle\qquad\geq\Omega(1)\cdot\min\left\{\frac{L_{1}\Delta}{\varepsilon^{2}}+\frac{L_{1}\Delta}{\varepsilon^{2}}\left(\frac{\sigma_{1}}{\varepsilon}\right)^{\frac{p}{p-1}},\left(\frac{\sigma_{1}}{\varepsilon}\right)^{\frac{p}{p-1}}+\frac{(L_{1}+\delta)\Delta}{\varepsilon^{2}}+\frac{\delta\Delta}{\varepsilon^{2}}\left(\frac{\sigma_{1}}{\varepsilon}\right)^{\frac{p}{q(p-1)}}\right\}.

It is worth noting that
2.6
is in fact equivalent to
2.7
and
2.2
. Specifically, the latter two assumptions imply
2.6
with
\bar{L}^{q}=2^{q-1}\left(L_{1}^{q}+\delta^{q}\right)
, while conversely, if
2.6
holds then both
2.2
and
2.7
hold with
L_{1}=\bar{L}
and
\delta=\bar{L}
. Hence,
L_{1}\leq\bar{L}
,
\delta\leq\bar{L}
, and
\bar{L}^{q}\leq 2^{q-1}\left(L_{1}^{q}+\delta^{q}\right)\leq 2^{q}\bar{L}^{q}
. Expressing the rate in terms of
L_{1}
and
\delta
is more precise than using
\bar{L}
alone; in particular, when
\delta\ll\bar{L}
, we show in
Theorem

4.2
that
NSGD-MVR
can achieve improved complexity, even matching the second term of the above
\min\left\{\ldots\right\}
.

3.3
Under Bounded Central Moments

Compared to the previous results, which focused on first-order methods under
q
-weak average smoothness and
(q,\delta)
–similarity, we consider here the setting where the oracle’s noise is controlled only via bounded central moments, allowing distinct exponents
p
and
q
for the stochastic gradients and Hessians.

Theorem 3.3
.

Given
\Delta,L_{1},L_{2}>0
,
\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2}\geq 0
and
0<\varepsilon\leq c_{1}\min\{\sqrt{L_{1}\Delta},L_{2}^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{3}}}\Delta^{\nicefrac{{2}}{{3}}}\}
for some universal constant
c_{1}>0
. Then, for any algorithm
A\in\mathcal{A}_{\texttt{zr}}
, there exists a function
f\in\mathcal{F}\left(\Delta,L_{1},L_{2}\right)
, an oracle and a distribution
(O,\mathcal{D})\in\mathcal{O}\left(f,\sigma_{1}^{p},\sigma_{2}^{q}\right)
such that
1
1

1

For clarity, we omit the deterministic term in this lower bound. The full lower bound can be found in the proof of
Theorem

3.3
in
Section

C.5
.

\displaystyle\mathfrak{m}^{\texttt{zr}}_{\varepsilon}\left(K,L_{1},L_{2},\Delta,\sigma_{1}^{p},\sigma_{2}^{q}\right)

\displaystyle\quad\geq\Omega(1)\cdot\min\left\{\frac{L_{1}\Delta}{\varepsilon^{2}}\left(\frac{\sigma_{1}}{\varepsilon}\right)^{\frac{p}{p-1}},\frac{L_{2}^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}\Delta}{\varepsilon^{\nicefrac{{3}}{{2}}}}\left(\frac{\sigma_{1}}{\varepsilon}\right)^{\frac{p}{p-1}},\left(\frac{\sigma_{1}}{\varepsilon}\right)^{\frac{p}{p-1}}+\frac{\sigma_{2}\Delta}{\varepsilon^{2}}+\frac{\sigma_{2}\Delta}{\varepsilon^{2}}\left(\frac{\sigma_{1}}{\varepsilon}\right)^{\frac{p}{q(p-1)}}\right\}.

Remark 3.1
.

In the noiseless setting, i.e.,
\sigma_{1}=\sigma_{2}=0
, the complexity of
Theorem

3.3
reduces to

\min\left\{\frac{L_{1}\Delta}{\varepsilon^{2}},\frac{L_{2}^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}\Delta}{\varepsilon^{\nicefrac{{3}}{{2}}}}\right\},

which is matched by the combination of gradient descent (
GD
) and cubic regularized Newton method
(
56
)
, hence is optimal. This is slightly better than the
\smash{\mathcal{O}(L_{1}^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}L_{2}^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{4}}}\Delta\varepsilon^{-\nicefrac{{7}}{{4}}})}
bound achieved in
14
, since

\min\left\{\frac{L_{1}\Delta}{\varepsilon^{2}},\frac{L_{2}^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}\Delta}{\varepsilon^{\nicefrac{{3}}{{2}}}}\right\}\leq\sqrt{\frac{L_{1}\Delta}{\varepsilon^{2}}\cdot\frac{L_{2}^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}\Delta}{\varepsilon^{\nicefrac{{3}}{{2}}}}}=\frac{L_{1}^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}L_{2}^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{4}}}\Delta}{\varepsilon^{\nicefrac{{7}}{{4}}}},

but achieving (
3.1
) requires full Hessian access in order to get rid of the dependency in
L_{1}

(
5
;
15
)
.

4
Optimal Method under First-Order
p
–BCM

To establish the optimal rate, we consider the well-known Normalized
SGD
algorithm with momentum variance reduction
MVR
technique
(
19
)
, and present its pseudo-code in
Algorithm

1
.

1

:

2

, the number of iterations

(Normalized

4.1
Convergence Analysis

4.1.1
Case of Known Tail Indices
p
and
q

Theorem 4.1
.

Under
2.1
,
2.3
and
2.6
, let the initial gradient estimate
g_{0}
be given by

g_{0}=\frac{1}{B_{\textnormal{init}}}\sum_{j=1}^{B_{\textnormal{init}}-1}\nabla f\left(x_{0},\xi_{0,j}\right),

where
B_{\textnormal{init}}=\max\left\{1,\left(\frac{\sigma_{1}}{\varepsilon}\right)^{\frac{p}{p-1}}\right\}
, let the stepsize
\gamma=\sqrt{\frac{\Delta\alpha^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{q}}}}{\bar{L}T}}
, the momentum parameter
\alpha=\min\left\{1,\alpha_{\textnormal{eff}}\right\}
where

\alpha_{\textnormal{eff}}=\max\left\{\left(\frac{\varepsilon}{\sigma_{1}T}\right)^{\frac{p}{2p-1}},\left(\frac{\bar{L}\Delta}{\sigma_{1}^{2}T}\right)^{\frac{pq}{p(2q+1)-2q}}\right\}.

Then,
Algorithm

1
guarantees to find an
\varepsilon
–stationary point with total sample complexity

\mathcal{O}\left(\left(\frac{\sigma_{1}}{\varepsilon}\right)^{\frac{p}{p-1}}+\frac{\bar{L}\Delta}{\varepsilon^{2}}+\frac{\bar{L}\Delta}{\varepsilon^{2}}\left(\frac{\sigma_{1}}{\varepsilon}\right)^{\frac{p}{q(p-1)}}\right).

The detailed proof is provided in
Appendix

D
.

Using
2.7
combined with
2.2
, we obtain the following refined result.

Theorem 4.2
.

Under
2.1
,
2.2
,
2.3
and
2.7
, let the initial gradient estimate
g_{0}
be given by

g_{0}=\frac{1}{B_{\textnormal{init}}}\sum_{j=1}^{B_{\textnormal{init}}-1}\nabla f\left(x_{0},\xi_{0,j}\right),

with
B_{\textnormal{init}}=\max\left\{1,\left(\frac{\sigma_{1}}{\varepsilon}\right)^{\frac{p}{p-1}}\right\}
, stepsize
\gamma=\min\left\{\sqrt{\frac{\Delta}{L_{1}T}},\sqrt{\frac{\Delta\alpha^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{q}}}}{\delta T}}\right\}
, momentum parameter
\alpha=\min\left\{1,\alpha_{\textnormal{eff}}\right\}
, where

\alpha_{\textnormal{eff}}=\max\left\{\left(\frac{\varepsilon}{\sigma_{1}T}\right)^{\frac{p}{2p-1}},\left(\frac{\delta\Delta}{\sigma_{1}^{2}T}\right)^{\frac{pq}{p(2q+1)-2q}}\right\}.

Then
Algorithm

1
is guaranteed to find an
\varepsilon
-stationary point with total sample complexity

\mathcal{O}\left(\left(\frac{\sigma_{1}}{\varepsilon}\right)^{\frac{p}{p-1}}+\frac{(L_{1}+\delta)\Delta}{\varepsilon^{2}}+\frac{\delta\Delta}{\varepsilon^{2}}\left(\frac{\sigma_{1}}{\varepsilon}\right)^{\frac{p}{q(p-1)}}\right).

The detailed proof is provided in
Appendix

D
. Essentially, the constant
L_{1}+\delta
is of same order as the constant
\bar{L}
from
2.6
.

This shows that the lower bound from
Theorem

3.2
is matched, up to some multiplicative constant, by the combination of
NSGD-Mom
(normalized
SGD
with momentum) with
NSGD-MVR
, which is
Algorithm

1
.

4.1.2
Case of Unknown Tail Indices
p
and
q

Theorem 4.3
.

Under
2.1
,
2.3
and
2.6
, assume

g_{0}=\nabla f(x_{0},\xi_{0})
,
\gamma=\sqrt{\frac{\Delta\alpha}{\bar{L}T}}
,

\alpha=T^{-\frac{1}{2}}\in\mathopen{(}0\mathclose{},1\mathclose{]}
. Then in
Algorithm

1
we have

\frac{1}{T}\sum_{t=0}^{T-1}\mathbb{E}\left[\left\|\nabla F(x_{t})\right\|\right]=\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{\sigma_{1}}{T^{\frac{p-1}{2p}}}+\frac{\sqrt{\bar{L}\Delta}}{T^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{4}}}}\right).

Our
Theorem

4.3
makes the pessimistic assumption
q=1
, under which we recover the usual
\mathcal{O}(\smash{T^{-\frac{p-1}{2p}}})
rate with unknown tail index
p

(
34
;
50
)
. The bound (
4.3
) can be slightly refined under
2.2
and
2.7
.

Theorem 4.4
.

Under
2.1
,
2.2
,
2.3
and
2.7
,
assume
g_{0}=\nabla f(x_{0},\xi_{0})
,

\gamma=\min\left\{\sqrt{\frac{\Delta}{L_{1}T}},\sqrt{\frac{\Delta\alpha}{\delta T}}\right\}
,

\alpha=T^{-\frac{1}{2}}\in\mathopen{(}0\mathclose{},1\mathclose{]}
. Then in
Algorithm

1
we have

\frac{1}{T}\sum_{t=0}^{T-1}\mathbb{E}\left[\left\|\nabla F(x_{t})\right\|\right]=\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{\sigma_{1}}{T^{\frac{p-1}{2p}}}+\frac{\sqrt{\delta\Delta}}{T^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{4}}}}+\frac{\sqrt{L_{1}\Delta}}{T^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}}\right).

The detailed proof is provided in
Appendix

D
.

4.2
Discussion of the Obtained Results

As we can see,
NSGD-MVR
achieves the optimal rate: our upper bounds in
Theorems

4.1
and
4.2
match the lower bounds in
Theorems

3.1
and
3.2
. For the standard case
q=2
, the stochastic terms are

\tfrac{\bar{L}\Delta}{\varepsilon^{2}}\!\left(\tfrac{\sigma_{1}}{\varepsilon}\right)^{\nicefrac{{p}}{{2(p-1)}}}\quad\text{and}\quad\tfrac{\delta\Delta}{\varepsilon^{2}}\!\left(\tfrac{\sigma_{1}}{\varepsilon}\right)^{\nicefrac{{p}}{{2(p-1)}}},

under
2.6
(
q
-WAS) and
2.7
(
(q,\delta)
-S), respectively. Compared to
48
, this yields faster rates under weaker assumptions.

In the case
p=q
, these stochastic terms coincide with those for
NSGDHess
in
65
under
2.5
:

\tfrac{\sigma_{2}\Delta}{\varepsilon^{2}}\!\left(\tfrac{\sigma_{1}}{\varepsilon}\right)^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{(p-1)}}}.

This relation follows since
2.7
implies
2.5
, and under certain conditions
\delta\leq\sigma_{2}
. Moreover, for empirical risk minimization with
p=q=2
, the two assumptions are equivalent
(
5
)
.

These insights motivate the study of a more general framework for second-order stochastic optimization.

5
Near-Optimal Method under
p
–BCM Gradients and
q
–BCM Hessians

Unlike Algorithm
1
, we consider a method, which employs Hessian–vector products instead of differences of stochastic gradients. This modification leads to the Hessian-corrected momentum (
Hess
) technique
(
74
;
66
)
.

1

Initialization

:

2

x_{0}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
, the starting point

3

T>0
, the number of iterations

4

g_{0}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
, an initial vector

5

\gamma>0
, the stepsize

6

\alpha\in\mathopen{(}0\mathclose{},1\mathclose{]}
, the momentum parameter for
Hess

7

8

9

x_{1}\leftarrow x_{0}-\gamma\frac{g_{0}}{\left\|g_{0}\right\|}

10

For

t=1,2,\ldots,T-1

do

11

Sample
q_{t}\sim\mathcal{U}\left(\mathopen{[}0\mathclose{},1\mathclose{]}\right)

12

\hat{x}_{t}\leftarrow q_{t}x_{t}+(1-q_{t})x_{t-1}

13

//
Apply
Hess
, here
\xi_{t},\hat{\xi}_{t}\sim\mathcal{D}
are independent.

14

g_{t}\leftarrow(1-\alpha)\left(g_{t-1}+\nabla^{2}f(\hat{x}_{t},\hat{\xi}_{t})(x_{t}-x_{t-1})\right)+\alpha\nabla f\left(x_{t},\xi_{t}\right)

15

//
Do one descent step.

16

x_{t+1}\leftarrow x_{t}-\gamma\frac{g_{t}}{\left\|g_{t}\right\|}

17

Output:

x_{T}

Algorithm 2

NSGD-
Hess
(Normalized
SGD
with Hessian-corrected Momentum)

Remark 5.1
.

Algorithm

2
is not new and has been considered in
65
. Here, we provide a refined analysis and improve over
65
. Note that
Algorithm

2
is
zero-respecting
(see
Definition

B.4
) since
\mathrm{supp}\,(\hat{x}_{t})\subseteq\mathrm{supp}\,(x_{t-1})\cup\mathrm{supp}\,(x_{t})
, where
\mathrm{supp}\,(x):=\left\{i\in[d]\,:\,x_{i}\neq 0\right\}
is the support of
x=(x_{1},\ldots,x_{d})\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
.

Additionally, the use of
\hat{x}_{t}
taken uniformly at random on the line
\mathopen{[}x_{t-1}\mathclose{},x_{t}\mathclose{]}
allows to use
Lemma

G.1
, which provides a better overall sample complexity than the one derived in
74
.

Theorem 5.1
.

Under
2.1
,
2.2
,
2.4
,
2.3
and
2.5
, let the initial gradient estimate
g_{0}
be given by

g_{0}=\frac{1}{B_{\textnormal{init}}}\sum_{j=1}^{B_{\textnormal{init}}-1}\nabla f\left(x_{0},\xi_{0,j}\right),

where
B_{\textnormal{init}}=\max\left\{1,\left(\frac{\sigma_{1}}{\varepsilon}\right)^{\frac{p}{p-1}}\right\}
, let the stepsize

\gamma=\min\left\{\sqrt{\frac{\Delta}{L_{1}T}},\sqrt{\frac{\Delta\alpha^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{q}}}}{\sigma_{2}T}},\sqrt[3]{\frac{\Delta\alpha^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}}{L_{2}T}}\right\},

the momentum parameter
\alpha=\min\left\{1,\alpha_{\textnormal{eff}}\right\}
, where

\displaystyle\alpha_{\textnormal{eff}}=

\displaystyle\max\left\{\left(\frac{\varepsilon}{\sigma_{1}T}\right)^{\frac{p}{2p-1}}\!,\left(\frac{\sigma_{2}\Delta}{\sigma_{1}^{2}T}\right)^{\frac{pq}{p(2q+1)-2q}}\!,\left(\frac{L_{2}^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}\Delta}{\sigma_{1}^{\nicefrac{{3}}{{2}}}T}\right)^{\frac{4p}{7p-6}}\right\}.

Then,
Algorithm

2
guarantees to find an
\varepsilon
--stationary point with the total sample complexity
2
2

2

For clarity, we omit the optimization terms in this lower bound. The full lower bound can be found in the proof of
Theorem

5.1
in
Section

E.2
.

\displaystyle\mathcal{O}\left(\left(\frac{\sigma_{1}}{\varepsilon}\right)^{\frac{p}{p-1}}+\frac{\Delta\sigma_{2}}{\varepsilon^{2}}+\frac{\Delta\sigma_{2}}{\varepsilon^{2}}\left(\frac{\sigma_{1}}{\varepsilon}\right)^{\frac{p}{q(p-1)}}+\frac{L_{2}^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}\Delta\sigma_{1}^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{4}}}}{\varepsilon^{\nicefrac{{7}}{{4}}}}\left(\frac{\sigma_{1}}{\varepsilon}\right)^{\frac{1}{4(p-1)}}\right).

The detailed proof is provided in
Appendix

E
.

6
Gradient Clipping for High-Probability Convergence

In this section we conduct high-probability analysis under heavy-tailed noise. Since we work under
2.3
, we incorporate gradient clipping in
Algorithm

1
; the pseudo-code of the new method is provided in
Algorithm

3
. Formally, a clipping operator (clipping for short) is defined as

{\texttt{clip}}(v,\lambda)=\min\left\{1,\frac{\lambda}{\left\|v\right\|}\right\}v\quad\text{ for any $v\neq 0$ in $\mathbb{R}^{d}$},

where
\lambda>0
is called clipping level/threshold. The proofs are deferred to
Appendix

F

1

Initialization

:

2

x_{0}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
, the starting point

3

T>0
, the number of iterations

4

g_{0}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
, an initial vector

5

\gamma>0
, the stepsize

6

\alpha\in\mathopen{(}0\mathclose{},1\mathclose{]}
, the momentum parameter for
MVR

7

\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}>0
, the clipping thresholds

8

9

10

x_{1}\leftarrow x_{0}-\gamma\frac{g_{0}}{\left\|g_{0}\right\|}

11

For

t=1,2,\ldots,T-1

do

12

//
Apply
MVR
with
clip
.

13

g_{t}\leftarrow(1-\alpha)\big(g_{t-1}+{\texttt{clip}}(\nabla f\left(x_{t},\xi_{t}\right)-\nabla f\left(x_{t-1},\xi_{t}\right),\lambda_{1})\big)+\alpha\,{\texttt{clip}}(\nabla f\left(x_{t},\xi_{t}\right),\lambda_{2})

14

//
Do one descent step.

15

x_{t+1}\leftarrow x_{t}-\gamma\frac{g_{t}}{\left\|g_{t}\right\|}

16

Output:

x_{T}

Algorithm 3

D-
clip
-NSGD-MVR
(Double Clipped Normalized
SGD
with
MVR
)

Algorithm

3
modifies
Algorithm

1
by applying clipping not only to the stochastic gradient — to control heavy-tailed noise — but also to the gradient difference term. This additional clipping step is the primary feature distinguishing our method from Accelerated NSGD with clipping and momentum
(
48
)
. Crucially, this modification enables us to establish high-probability guarantees under assumptions weaker than those in
48
. While their analysis relies on individual smoothness (i.e., assuming
f(\cdot,\xi)
is
\bar{L}
-smooth almost surely), our analysis holds under
q
-Weak Average Smoothness (
2.6
). Individual smoothness is a significantly stronger condition; notably, under that assumption alone:
43
showed it is possible to attain an
\mathcal{O}(\varepsilon^{-4})
rate independent of the heavy-tail index
p
.

Theorem 6.1
.

Under
2.1
,
2.3
and
2.6
, let
T\geq 1
and
\beta\in\mathopen{(}0\mathclose{},1\mathclose{]}
be such that
\log\frac{8T}{\beta}\geq 1
. Let
x_{0}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
and define
\Delta:=F(x_{0})-F^{\inf}
.

Suppose that
Algorithm

3
is run with
g_{0}=0
, momentum parameter
\alpha=\max\{T^{-\frac{p}{2p-1}},T^{-\frac{pq}{p(2q+1)-2q}}\}
, clipping thresholds
\lambda_{1}=2\gamma\bar{L}\alpha^{-\frac{1}{q}}
and
\lambda_{2}=\max\{4\sqrt{\bar{L}\Delta_{1}},\sigma_{1}\alpha^{-\frac{1}{p}}\}
, and stepsize

\displaystyle\gamma

\displaystyle=\mathcal{O}\left(\min\left\{\sqrt{\frac{\Delta}{\bar{L}T}},\alpha\sqrt{\frac{\Delta}{\bar{L}}},\frac{1}{\alpha T\log\frac{T}{\beta}}\sqrt{\frac{\Delta}{\bar{L}}},\frac{\Delta_{1}}{\sigma_{1}\alpha^{\frac{p-1}{p}}T\log\frac{T}{\beta}},\sqrt{\frac{\Delta\alpha^{\frac{1}{q}}}{\bar{L}T\log\frac{T}{\beta}}}\right\}\right).

Then, with probability at least
1-\beta
, the output of
Algorithm

3
satisfies

\frac{1}{T}\sum_{t=0}^{T-1}\left\|\nabla F(x_{t})\right\|\leq\frac{2\Delta}{\gamma T},

and, by our choice of parameters, the gradient norm converges with high probability at the rate

\frac{1}{T}\sum_{t=0}^{T-1}\left\|\nabla F(x_{t})\right\|=\mathcal{O}\left(\left(\frac{\sqrt{\bar{L}\Delta}+\sigma_{1}}{T^{\min\left\{\frac{p-1}{2p-1},~\frac{q(p-1)}{p(2q+1)-2q}\right\}}}\right)\log\frac{T}{\beta}\right).

The detailed proof is provided in
Appendix

F
.

Notably, in the case where
p=q
, we establish the same rate
\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}\left(\varepsilon^{\frac{2p-1}{p-1}}\right)
as
48
did for Algorithm 2, but under weaker assumptions. Moreover, our lower bounds (see
Theorem

3.1
) indicate that this rate is optimal in terms of the dependence on
\varepsilon
.

Another interesting observation is that when
p\leq q
, the high-probability rate remains identical to the
p=q
case. In contrast, for in-expectation guarantees, we observe a faster rate (see
Theorem

4.1
). Determining whether this gap is fundamental or a limitation of the current proof technique remains an open question, and calls for a tighter high-probability analysis for these methods.

Furthermore,
Appendix

F
establishes high-probability convergence guarantees for
Algorithm

3
under
2.7
, and for
Algorithm

4
under
2.4
and
2.5
.

Appendix A
Additional Notation

Following
5
, a
q^{\textnormal{th}}
-order (
q\geq 0
) tensor
T\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times\cdots\times d}=\mathbb{R}^{\otimes^{q}d}
is a
q
-dimensional array of real numbers, where
\mathbb{R}^{\otimes^{q}d}
denotes the
q
-fold tensor product of
\mathbb{R}^{d}
. By convention, a
0^{\textnormal{th}}
-order tensor corresponds to a scalar (i.e., an element of
\mathbb{R}
), a
1^{\textnormal{st}}
-order tensor corresponds to a vector in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
, and a
2^{\textnormal{nd}}
-order tensor corresponds to a matrix in
\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}
. If
q\geq 1
, we denote
T=(T_{1},\ldots,T_{d})
where each
T_{i}
is the
(q-1)^{\textnormal{th}}
-order subtensor of
T
obtained by fixing the first index to
i
. Formally, for all
i\in[d]
, we define
[T_{i}]_{j_{1},\ldots,j{q-1}}=T_{i,j_{1},\ldots,j_{q-1}}
where
j_{1},\ldots,j_{q-1}\in[d]
. This recursive definition allows us to view any tensor as an ordered collection of its subtensors along a given mode. When
q=2
, i.e., when
T
is a matrix, we write
[T]_{i,\cdot}
for its
i^{\textnormal{th}}
row to avoid confusion with the
i^{\textnormal{th}}
coordinate of a vector. Similarly,
[T]_{\cdot,j}
denotes its
j^{\textnormal{th}}
column. Throughout this work, we only consider tensors of order
1
and
2
: first-order tensors correspond to gradients, and second-order tensors correspond to Hessians.

For any integer
n>0
, we define the index set
[n]:=\left\{1,2,\ldots,n\right\}
. Let
d\geq 1
denote the ambient dimension. We use
\left\langle\cdot,\cdot\right\rangle
to represent the standard Euclidean inner product on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
, i.e.,
\left\langle x,y\right\rangle=\sum_{i=1}^{d}x_{i}y_{i}
, and
\left\|\cdot\right\|
to denote the associated
\ell^{2}
–norm,
\left\|x\right\|=\sqrt{\left\langle x,x\right\rangle}
. For a matrix
A\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}
, we denote by
\left\|A\right\|_{\text{op}}
its operator (spectral) norm, defined as
\left\|A\right\|_{\text{op}}=\sup_{\left\|x\right\|=1}\left\|Ax\right\|
. We write
\nabla f(\cdot,\cdot)
and
\nabla^{2}f(\cdot,\cdot)
for the stochastic gradient and Hessian oracles, respectively. For any two real numbers
a,b
, we use
a\wedge b:=\min\left\{a,b\right\}
and
a\vee b:=\max\left\{a,b\right\}
. We adopt the standard asymptotic notations
\mathcal{O}(\cdot)
and
\Omega(\cdot)
to denote upper and lower bounds on growth rates. To prevent ambiguity, constants related to first-order (gradient) quantities are indexed by the subscript
1
(e.g.,
L_{1}
,
\sigma_{1}
), whereas those related to second-order (Hessian) quantities carry the subscript
2
(e.g.,
L_{2}
,
\sigma_{2}
).

We denote by
\mathbb{P}\left(E\right)
the probability of an event
E
and by
\mathbb{E}\left[X\right]
the expectation of a random variable
X
. Conditional probability and conditional expectation are written, respectively, as
\mathop{\mathbb{P}}\left(E\,|\,\mathcal{F}\right)
and
{\mathbb{E}}\left[\left.X\,\right|\,\mathcal{F}\right]
, where
\mathcal{F}
denotes a
\sigma
–algebra or a conditioning event. The notation
{\mathbb{E}}_{\xi}\left[\cdot\right]
(or equivalently
{\mathbb{E}}_{\xi\sim\mathbb{P}}\left[\cdot\right]
) indicates that the expectation is taken with respect to the randomness of
\xi
, and explicitly that
\xi
is distributed according to the probability law
\mathbb{P}
. This notation is used to clarify the source of randomness when multiple random variables are involved or when the distribution of
\xi
is not immediately clear from the context.

Appendix B
Additional Definitions

We present in this section the formal setup (function, oracle and algorithm/protocol classes) we considered and under which our (dimension-free) lower and upper bounds are derived.

B.1
The Setup

B.1.1
Function Class

The lower bounds developed in this work apply to algorithms that find
\varepsilon
–stationary point of (nonconvex) functions. All functions considered here are defined from
\mathbb{R}^{d}
to
\mathbb{R}
where
d\geq 1
is an integer. Depending on the assumptions (
2.6
,
2.7
and
2.4
), the class of functions may vary. Formally, we define

\mathcal{F}(\Delta):=\left\{F\in\mathcal{C}^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d},\mathbb{R})\;\middle|\;F(0)-\inf_{x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}F(x)\leq\Delta\right\}

which is the class of continuously differentiable and
\Delta
–bounded functions. If the considered function further satisfies some (standard) regularity conditions (see
2.2
and
2.4
) we let

\mathcal{F}(\Delta,L_{1}):=\left\{F\in\mathcal{C}^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d},\mathbb{R})\;\middle|\;\begin{aligned} &F(0)-\inf_{x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}F(x)\leq\Delta,\\
&\left\|\nabla F(x)-\nabla F(y)\right\|\leq L_{1}\left\|x-y\right\|,\quad\forall x,y\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\end{aligned}\right\}

and

\mathcal{F}(\Delta,L_{1},L_{2}):=\left\{F\in\mathcal{C}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d},\mathbb{R})\;\middle|\;\begin{aligned} &F(0)-\inf_{x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}F(x)\leq\Delta,\\
&\left\|\nabla F(x)-\nabla F(y)\right\|\leq L_{1}\left\|x-y\right\|,\quad\forall x,y\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\\
&\left\|\nabla^{2}F(x)-\nabla^{2}F(y)\right\|_{\text{op}}\leq L_{2}\left\|x-y\right\|,\quad\forall x,y\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\end{aligned}\right\}

when access to second-order information is possible. The Lipchitz constants
L_{1}
and
L_{2}
appearing above are measured with respect to the canonical (Euclidean)
\ell^{2}
–norm over
\mathbb{R}^{d}
for the gradients and points while, for the Hessian we use the operator norm
\left\|\cdot\right\|_{\text{op}}
induced by
\left\|\cdot\right\|
. We recall in
Definition

H.1
the definition of
\left\|\cdot\right\|_{\text{op}}
.

The dimension
d\geq 1
appearing in the above definitions will be made explicit in the proofs (see
Appendix

C
) and it may depends on some problem specific parameters, e.g.,
\varepsilon
,
\Delta
and the smoothness constants.

Note that the function class (
B.1.1
) as stated is too broad and lacks regularity, e.g., Lipchitz continuous gradients. Nonetheless in some cases, appropriate assumptions on the stochastic gradients (for instance,
mean-squared smoothness
, see
2.6
) are enough to ensure the underlying function itself is smooth, i.e., belongs to
\mathcal{F}(\Delta,L)
for some constant
L\geq 0
(for the exact derivation of this fact, see (
65
)).

B.1.2
Oracle Class

Definition B.1

(Stochastic
p^{\textnormal{th}}
-order Oracles
(
5
)
)
.

Given integers
d,p\geq 1
and a function
F\in\mathcal{C}^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d},\mathbb{R})
, we define
\mathcal{O}_{p}(F)
as the class stochastic
p^{\textnormal{th}}
-order oracles, i.e., the pairs
(\mathbb{P}_{\xi},\texttt{O}^{p}_{F})
where
\mathbb{P}_{\xi}
is a distribution on a measurable set
\mathcal{Z}
and
\texttt{O}^{p}_{F}
is an unbiased mapping defined as

\texttt{O}^{p}_{F}\colon(x,\xi)\mapsto\left(F(x),\nabla f(x,\xi),\ldots,\nabla^{p}f(x,\xi)\right);

that is, for every
r\in[p]
we have

{\mathbb{E}}_{\xi\sim\mathbb{P}_{\xi}}\left[\nabla^{r}f(x,\xi)\right]=\nabla^{r}F(x).

Furthermore, if some of the derivative estimator (here, only the gradient estimator
\nabla f(x,\xi)
or the Hessian estimator
\nabla^{2}f(x,\xi)
are used) satisfies additional properties or assumption depending on some parameters
\sigma_{1}
(
2.3
),
\sigma_{2}
(
2.5
),
\bar{L}
(
2.6
),
\delta
(
2.7
) and so on, the oracle class will be denoted by

\mathcal{O}_{p}(F,\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2}\ldots),

with all parameters listed inside the parenthesis, in an arbitrary order. As each assumption defines unambiguously its own parameters, we keep the notation (
B.1.2
) for simplicity as it avoids any risk of ambiguity.

B.1.3
Optimization Protocol and Algorithm Class

First, let us introduce some important definitions.

Definition B.2

(Support of a Vector/Tensor)
.

Let
d\geq 1
be an integer, the support of a vector
x=(x_{1},\ldots,x_{d})\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
is the set

\mathrm{supp}\,(x):=\left\{i\in[d]\,:\,x_{i}\neq 0\right\},

i.e., the set of all indices
i\in[d]
for which
x
has a nonzero
i^{\textnormal{th}}
coordinate.

For a given
p^{\textnormal{th}}
-order tensor
T=(T_{1},\ldots,T_{d})\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times\cdots\times d}=\mathbb{R}^{\otimes^{p}d}
, its support is defined as

\mathrm{supp}\,(T):=\left\{i\in[d]\,:\,T_{i}\neq 0\right\},

where
T_{i}
is the
i^{\textnormal{th}}
sub-tensor (which is a
(p-1)^{\textnormal{th}}
-order tensor), e.g., the
i^{\textnormal{th}}
row of the matrix
T
if
p=2
.

Definition B.3

(Progress, “
\mathrm{prog}\,
”)
.

Let
d\geq 1
be an integer, for any
x=(x_{1},\ldots,x_{d})\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
and any
\alpha\in\mathopen{[}0\mathclose{},+\infty\mathclose{)}
we define

\mathrm{prog}\,_{\alpha}(x):=\max\left\{i\in[d]\,:\,\left\lvert x_{i}\right\rvert>\alpha\right\},

and
\mathrm{prog}\,_{\alpha}(x)=0
if
\left\lvert x_{i}\right\rvert\leq\alpha
for all
i\in[d]
.

If
\alpha=0
, for a given
p^{\textnormal{th}}
-order tensor
T=(T_{1},\ldots,T_{d})\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times\cdots\times d}=\mathbb{R}^{\otimes^{p}d}
, its “
\mathrm{prog}\,
” is defined as

\mathrm{prog}\,_{0}(T):=\max\left\{i\in[d]\,:\,T_{i}\neq 0\right\},

or
0
if no there is not such index
i\in[d]
for which
T_{i}\neq 0
.

Notably, if
\alpha=0
then
\mathrm{prog}\,_{0}(x)
is the largest index at which
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
has a nonzero coordinate.
\mathrm{prog}\,_{0}
will be used to capture the rate at which new coordinates are “discovered”. Initially, all coordinates are set to
0
and, as we progressively acquire information from the queries to the oracle, the union of the support of the oracle responses grows. The growth rate is quantified using
\mathrm{prog}\,
and controlled thanks to the notion os
zero-chain
, which we recall formally below in
Definitions

B.5
and
B.6
.

We recall below some technical notions from the paper of
4
.

Optimization Protocol:

the lower bound guarantees obtained in this work apply to algorithms interacting with an oracle over several rounds, where in each round they may issue a batch of
K\geq 1
queries (“
multi-point
” queries). More formally, at round
i\geq 1
, the algorithm queries the oracle at a batch
\smash{\mathbf{x}^{(i)}=(x^{(i)}_{1},\ldots,x^{(i)}_{K})}
of
K
points in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
and for each point
\smash{x^{(i)}_{j}}
,
j\in[K]
, the oracle performs an independent draw
\xi^{(i)}\sim\mathbb{P}_{\xi}
and replies with

\texttt{O}_{F}^{p,K}\left(\mathbf{x}^{(i)},\xi^{(i)}\right):=\left(\texttt{O}^{p}_{F}\left(x^{(i)}_{1},\xi^{(i)}\right),\ldots,\texttt{O}^{p}_{F}\left(x^{(i)}_{K},\xi^{(i)}\right)\right),

where the randomness is shared across all queries of the batch: the same seed
\smash{\xi^{(i)}}
is used. For instance, in
Algorithm

1
we use a batch a
K=2
queries.

Optimization Algorithms:

a (randomized) algorithm
A
consists of a distribution
\mathbb{P}_{r}
(over a measurable set
\mathcal{R}
), a random seed
r\sim\mathbb{P}_{r}
drawn at the very beginning of the protocol, and a sequence of measurable mappings
\{\texttt{A}^{(i)}\}_{i\geq 1}
such that
\smash{\texttt{A}^{(i)}}
takes all the previous
i-1
oracle responses and use the random seed
r\in\mathcal{R}
to produce the next
i^{\textnormal{th}}
query. Formally, a randomized algorithm
A
produces the sequence of iterates
\{\mathbf{x}_{\texttt{A}[\texttt{O}^{p,K}_{F}]}^{(i)}\}_{i\geq 1}
where

\mathbf{x}^{(i)}_{\texttt{A}[\texttt{O}^{p,K}_{F}]}:=\texttt{A}^{(i)}\left(\left[\texttt{O}_{F}^{p,K}\left(\mathbf{x}_{\texttt{A}[\texttt{O}^{p,K}_{F}]}^{(1)},\xi^{(1)}\right),\ldots,\texttt{O}_{F}^{p,K}\left(\mathbf{x}_{\texttt{A}[\texttt{O}^{p,K}_{F}]}^{(i-1)},\xi^{(i-1)}\right)\right],r\right).

We define
\mathcal{A}_{\texttt{rand}}(K)
as the class of all algorithms that follow the aforementioned protocol with a batch size of
K
queries per round.

Definition B.4

(Zero-Respecting Algorithm
(
4
;
5
, Definition 1)
)
.

A stochastic
p^{\textnormal{th}}
-order algorithm
A
is
zero-respecting
if for any function
F\in\mathcal{C}^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d},\mathbb{R})
and any
p^{\textnormal{th}}
-order oracle
\texttt{O}^{p,K}_{F}
, the iterates
\{\mathbf{x}_{\texttt{A}[\texttt{O}^{p,K}_{F}]}^{(i)}\}_{i\geq 1}
satisfies, for any

\mathrm{supp}\,\left(\mathbf{x}_{\texttt{A}[\texttt{O}^{p,K}_{F}]}^{(i)}\right)\subseteq\bigcup_{j=1}^{i-1}\mathrm{supp}\,\left(\texttt{O}_{F}^{p,K}\left(\mathbf{x}_{\texttt{A}[\texttt{O}^{p,K}_{F}]}^{(j)},\xi^{(j)}\right)\right),

We define
\mathcal{A}_{\texttt{zr}}(K)\subseteq\mathcal{A}_{\texttt{rand}}(K)
as the class of all
zero-respecting
algorithms.

In other words, a zero-respecting algorithm cannot modify coordinates where no information is known; that is, its queries at each round have support in the union of the supports of all previous oracle responses.

In the rest of the paper, we drop the
K
in all our notation for simplicity and because it does not appear in any of the lower bound complexity we derive.

Proof Strategy: Zero-Chains

The main motivations for building the hard instance (
B.2
) is the notion of zero-chain, which in some sense, are functions for which it is “hard” to uncover new, nonzero, coordinates.

Definition B.5

(Deterministic
p^{\textnormal{th}}
-order Zero-Chain
(
13
, Definition 3)
)
.

Given an integer
p\geq 1
and a function
F\in\mathcal{C}^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d},\mathbb{R})
, we say that
F
is a
p^{\textnormal{th}}
-order zero-chain
if, for every
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
and every
i\in[d]
we have

\mathrm{supp}\,(x)\subseteq\left\{1,2,\ldots,i-1\right\}\,\,\text{ implies }\,\,\bigcup_{r=1}^{p}\mathrm{supp}\,(\nabla^{r}F(x))\subseteq\left\{1,2,\ldots,i\right\}.

We say that
F
is a
zero-chain
if it is a
p^{\textnormal{th}}
-order zero-chain for every integer
p\geq 1
.

In other word,
Definition

B.5
tells us that given
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
, we can “discover” at most one new coordinate when accessing the gradient of
F
at
x
(or any high-order derivatives of
F
). When dealing with stochastic estimators instead, we can extend the previous
Definition

B.5
as follows:

Definition B.6

(Probability-
\theta

p^{\textnormal{th}}
-order Zero-Chain
(
5
, Definition 2)
)
.

Given an integer
p\geq 1
,
\theta\in\mathopen{(}0\mathclose{},1\mathclose{]}
, a function
F\in\mathcal{C}^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d},\mathbb{R})
and derivative estimators
\nabla f(x,\xi)
,
\ldots
,
\nabla^{p}f(x,\xi)
of
F
, we say that
F
is a probability-
\theta

p^{\textnormal{th}}
-order zero-chain
if we have

\mathbb{P}\left(\exists x_{0}\mid\mathrm{prog}\,_{0}\left(\nabla f(x_{0},\xi),\ldots,\nabla^{p}f(x_{0},\xi)\right)>\mathrm{prog}\,_{\alpha}(x_{0})+1\right)=0,

and

\mathbb{P}\left(\exists x_{0}\mid\mathrm{prog}\,_{0}\left(\nabla f(x_{0},\xi),\ldots,\nabla^{p}f(x_{0},\xi)\right)=\mathrm{prog}\,_{\alpha}(x_{0})+1\right)\leq\theta,

We say that
F
is a
probability-
\theta
zero-chain
if it is a probability-
\theta

p^{\textnormal{th}}
-order zero-chain for every integer
p\geq 1
.

In the above
Definition

B.6
, condition (
B.6
) is the analogue of condition (
B.5
) when we have only access to noisy derivatives estimators of
F
. In addition, condition (
B.6
) tells us that we have a “small” chance to discover a new coordinate upon querying the oracle, i.e., the added noise behaves “adversarially” and can slow down the discovery of new coordinates (in expectation).

An important properties of probability-
\theta
zero-chain is the following lemma.

Lemma B.1

(
5
)
.

Let
p\geq 1
,
\theta\in\mathopen{(}0\mathclose{},1\mathclose{]}
, a function
F\in\mathcal{C}^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d},\mathbb{R})
and unbiased derivative estimators
\nabla f(x,\xi)
,
\ldots
,
\nabla^{p}f(x,\xi)
of
F
which form a probability-
\theta
zero-chain and let
{\texttt{O}^{p}_{F}}
be an oracle such that
{\texttt{O}^{p}_{F}}(x,\xi)=\left(F(x),\nabla f(x,\xi),\ldots,\nabla^{p}f(x,\xi)\right)
.

Let
\{\mathbf{x}_{{\texttt{A}}[{\texttt{O}^{p}_{F}}]}^{(i)}\}_{i\geq 1}
be the queries produced by any zero-respecting algorithm
{\texttt{A}}\in\mathcal{A}_{\texttt{zr}}
interacting with
{\texttt{O}^{p}_{F}}
. Then, with probability at least
1-\delta
, we have

\mathrm{prog}\,_{0}\left(\mathbf{x}_{{\texttt{A}}[{\texttt{O}^{p}_{F}}]}^{(t)}\right)<T\,\,\text{ for all }\,\,t\leq\frac{T-\log(\frac{1}{\delta})}{2\theta}.

The proof can be found in
5
and
6
.

B.1.4
Complexity Measures

As in
4
;
5
, we develop lower bounds on the
distributional complexity
for finding an
\varepsilon
–stationary point, which in turn, implies lower bounds on the
minimax complexity
for finding such stationary points. Formally, following
4

\mathfrak{m}^{\texttt{zr}}_{\varepsilon}(K,\Delta\ldots):=\sup_{F\in\mathcal{F}(\Delta\ldots)}\sup_{(\mathbb{P}_{\xi},\texttt{O}_{F})\in\mathcal{O}(F\ldots)}\inf_{\texttt{A}\in\mathcal{A}_{\texttt{zr}}}\inf\left\{T\geq 1\,:\,{\mathbb{E}}_{\texttt{O}_{F},\texttt{A}}\left[\left\|\nabla F\left(\mathbf{x}_{{\texttt{A}}[{\texttt{O}_{F}}]}^{(T)}\right)\right\|\right]\leq\varepsilon\right\},

where the expectation is taken over the randomness in the oracle
\texttt{O}_{F}
and in the algorithm
\texttt{A}\in\mathcal{A}_{\texttt{zr}}
, if any.

As in the definition of the oracle class, complexity measures may depend on various parameters depending on the assumptions considered. The convention is to list all involved parameters (in an arbitrary order), and there is no ambiguity in doing so.

B.2
The Worst-Case Function

In this section, we recall the “worst-case” function introduced in
13
;
4
and which is used to prove our lower bounds. This function (or some variations, depending on the targeted class of functions) is at the core of many lower bounds in stochastic nonvonvex optimization
(
4
;
77
;
76
;
36
;
73
;
65
)
. Formally, given an integer
T\geq 1
which denotes the dimension in which the hard instance
F_{T}\colon\mathbb{R}^{T}\to\mathbb{R}
lies, we define

F_{T}\colon x\mapsto-\Psi(1)\Phi(x_{1})+\sum_{i=2}^{T}\left[\Psi(-x_{i-1})\Phi(-x_{i})-\Psi(x_{i-1})\Phi(x_{i})\right],

where the inner components
\Psi
and
\Phi
are defined, for any
t\in\mathbb{R}
, as

\Psi(t):=\begin{cases}0,&\text{if $t\leq\frac{1}{2}$;}\\
\exp\left(1-\frac{1}{(2t-1)^{2}}\right),&\text{if $t>\frac{1}{2}$;}\end{cases}\quad\text{ and }\quad\Phi(t):=\sqrt{e}\int_{-\infty}^{t}e^{-\frac{s^{2}}{2}}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}s.

The particular design (
B.2
) of
F_{T}
is such that it is a (deterministic) zero-chain (see
Definition

B.5
) and the gradients of
F_{T}
are large unless all coordinates are large (i.e.,
\mathrm{prog}\,_{1}(x)\geq T
); these two keys properties are recalled below (
5.
and
6.
). Several other properties of the function
F_{T}
are stated in the next lemma.

Lemma B.2

(Properties of the Hard Instance
(
13
;
4
)
)
.

For any integer
T\geq 1
, the function
F_{T}
satisfies:

1.

F_{T}(0)-\inf_{x\in\mathbb{R}^{T}}F_{T}(x)\leq\Delta_{0}T
where
\Delta_{0}:=12
,

2.

The gradient of
F_{T}
is
\ell_{1}
–Lipschitz continuous with
\ell_{1}:=152
,

3.

For all
x\in\mathbb{R}^{T}
, we have
\left\|\nabla F_{T}(x)\right\|_{\infty}\leq\gamma_{\infty}
where
\gamma_{\infty}:=23
. Notably, this shows that
\left\|\nabla F_{T}(x)\right\|\leq 23\sqrt{T}
,

4.

There exists an universal constant
0<c<+\infty
such that for every integer
p\geq 1
, the
p
–th order derivatives of
F_{T}
are
\ell_{p}
–Lipschitz continuous for some
\ell_{p}\leq\exp\left(\frac{5}{2}p\log p+cp\right)
,

5.

For all
x\in\mathbb{R}^{T}
we have
\mathrm{prog}\,_{0}(\nabla F_{T}(x))\leq\mathrm{prog}\,_{\frac{1}{2}}(x)+1
,

6.

For all
x\in\mathbb{R}^{T}
, if
\mathrm{prog}\,_{0}(x)<T
then
\left\|\nabla F_{T}(x)\right\|>1
.

In the proofs of our lower bounds, we mostly focus on properties
1.
,
2.
,
3.
and
4.
which, after a specific rescaling, are enough to ensure the hard instance
F_{T}
belongs to the right class of functions. The two remaining properties
5.
and
6.
are not explicitly used in this paper but their role, however, should not be underestimated: they guarantee that the inner mechanics of the hard instance of
13
;
4
remain the same, thereby ensuring that any zero-respecting algorithm on
F_{T}
behaves as intended and that their lower-bound strategy carries over.

B.3
The Stochastic Gradient and Hessian Estimator

We recall below from
4
;
5
the gradient and Hessian estimators used:

Definition B.7

(Gradient Estimator)
.

Given an integer
T\geq 1
and
\theta\in\mathopen{(}0\mathclose{},1\mathclose{]}
, the gradient estimator
g_{T}\colon\mathbb{R}^{T}\times\mathcal{Z}\to\mathbb{R}^{T}
of the hard instance
F_{T}
in (
B.2
) is defined coordinate-wise as

\left[g_{T}(x,\xi)\right]_{i}:=\left[\nabla F_{T}(x)\right]_{i}\cdot\left(1+\mathbb{I}\!\left\{i>\mathrm{prog}\,_{\frac{1}{4}}(x)\right\}\left(\frac{\xi}{\theta}-1\right)\right),

where
i\in[T]
,
x\in\mathbb{R}^{T}
and
\xi\sim\mathrm{Ber}(\theta)
, i.e., we take as distribution
\mathbb{P}_{\xi}=\mathrm{Ber}(\theta)
and measurable set
\mathcal{Z}=\left\{0,1\right\}
.

Definition B.8

(Hessian Estimator)
.

Given an integer
T\geq 1
and
\theta\in\mathopen{(}0\mathclose{},1\mathclose{]}
, the Hessian estimator
\nabla g_{T}\colon\mathbb{R}^{T}\times\mathcal{Z}\to\mathbb{R}^{T}
of the hard instance
F_{T}
in (
B.2
) is defined row-wise as

\left[\nabla g_{T}(x,\xi)\right]_{i,\cdot}:=\left(1+\mathbb{I}\!\left\{i>\mathrm{prog}\,_{\frac{1}{4}}(x)\right\}\left(\frac{\xi}{\theta}-1\right)\right)\cdot\left[\nabla^{2}F_{T}(x)\right]_{i,\cdot},

where
i\in[T]
,
x\in\mathbb{R}^{T}
and
\xi\sim\mathrm{Ber}(\theta)
(as before).

Note that, for all
x\in\mathbb{R}^{T}
and all
i>\mathrm{prog}\,_{\frac{1}{4}}(x)+1
we have
\left[\nabla F_{T}(x)\right]_{i}=0
by
Lemma

B.2
(property
5.
) so, only the specific coordinate at
i=\mathrm{prog}\,_{\frac{1}{4}}(x)+1
is noisy.

In the case of
2.6
and
2.7
, i.e., additional regularity conditions on the gradient estimator, we define, as in
4
the “smoothed” indicator
\Theta_{i}
of
\mathbb{I}\!\left\{i>\mathrm{prog}\,_{\frac{1}{4}}(\cdot)\right\}
as:

\Theta_{i}\colon x\mapsto\Gamma\left(1-\left(\sum_{k=i}^{T}\Gamma\left(\left\lvert x_{k}\right\rvert\right)^{2}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}\right)=\Gamma\left(1-\left\|\left(\Gamma(\left\lvert x_{i}\right\rvert),\ldots,\Gamma(\left\lvert x_{T}\right\rvert)\right)\right\|\right),

where
\Gamma\colon\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
is any
\mathcal{C}^{\infty}
, non-decreasing Lipschitz continuous function with
\Gamma(t)=0
for all
t\leq\frac{1}{4}
and
\Gamma(t)=1
for all
t\geq\frac{1}{2}
. For instance, we can take, as in
4

\Gamma(t):=\frac{\int_{\frac{1}{4}}^{t}\Lambda(s)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}s}{\int_{\frac{1}{4}}^{\frac{1}{2}}\Lambda(s)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}s},\,\,\text{ where }\,\,\Lambda(t):=\begin{cases}0,&\text{if $t\leq\frac{1}{4}$ or $t\geq\frac{1}{2}$;}\\
\exp\left(-\frac{1}{100\left(t-\frac{1}{4}\right)\left(\frac{1}{2}-t\right)}\right),&\text{if $\frac{1}{4}<t<\frac{1}{2}$;}\end{cases}

for any
t\in\mathbb{R}
. Notably, the smoothed indicator
\Theta_{i}
satisfies for all
x\in\mathbb{R}^{T}

\mathbb{I}\!\left\{i>\mathrm{prog}\,_{\frac{1}{4}}(x)\right\}\leq\Theta_{i}(x)\leq\mathbb{I}\!\left\{i>\mathrm{prog}\,_{\frac{1}{2}}(x)\right\},

hence

\Theta_{i}(x)=\begin{cases}1,&\text{for all $i>\mathrm{prog}\,_{\frac{1}{4}}(x)$;}\\
0,&\text{for all $i\leq\mathrm{prog}\,_{\frac{1}{2}}(x)$.}\end{cases}

Overall, this gives the following “smoothed” gradient estimator:

Definition B.9

(“Smoothed” Gradient Estimator)
.

Given an integer
T\geq 1
and
\theta\in\mathopen{(}0\mathclose{},1\mathclose{]}
, the gradient estimator
g_{T}\colon\mathbb{R}^{T}\times\mathcal{Z}\to\mathbb{R}^{T}
of the hard instance
F_{T}
in (
B.2
) is defined coordinate-wise as

\left[g_{T}(x,\xi)\right]_{i}:=\left[\nabla F_{T}(x)\right]_{i}\cdot\left(1+\Theta_{i}(x)\left(\frac{\xi}{\theta}-1\right)\right),

where
i\in[T]
,
x\in\mathbb{R}^{T}
and
\xi\sim\mathrm{Ber}(\theta)
(as before).

The gradient and Hessian estimators defined above are all probability-
\theta
zero-chain.

Appendix C
Proofs of the Lower Bounds

C.1
A Lower Bound in the
Global Stochastic Model
Under Heavy-Tail Noise

In this part, we extend
4
in the setting where we only assume the variance of the gradient estimator to have bounded
p
–th moment for some
p\in\mathopen{(}1\mathclose{},2\mathclose{]}
. This result holds in any dimension
d\geq 1
.

Lemma C.1
.

Under
2.1
,
2.3
and
2.6
and as long as
0<\varepsilon\leq\frac{1}{8}\sqrt{\bar{L}\Delta}
, the number of samples required to obtain an
\varepsilon
–stationary point in the global stochastic model defined above is

\Omega(1)\cdot\left(\frac{\sigma_{1}}{\varepsilon}\right)^{\frac{p}{p-1}},

where
p\in\mathopen{(}1\mathclose{},2\mathclose{]}
.

In particular, this lower bound does not depend on the exponent
q
from
2.6
.

Remark C.1
.

More precisely,
Lemma

C.1
shows that

\mathfrak{m}^{\texttt{zr}}_{\varepsilon}\left(K,\bar{L},\Delta,\sigma_{1}^{p}\right)\geq\Omega(1)\cdot\max\left\{1,\left(\frac{\sigma_{1}}{\varepsilon}\right)^{\frac{p}{p-1}}\right\}.

Remark C.2
.

It is also worth mentioning that the same lower bound holds under
2.1
,
2.2
,
2.3
and
2.7
since, by our choice of function (
C.1
) for any
s\in\left\{-1,1\right\}
and any
x,y\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
we have

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}_{\xi\sim\mathbb{P}^{s}}\left[\left\|\left[\nabla f_{d}^{\star}(x,\xi)-\nabla f_{d}^{\star}(y,\xi)\right]-\left[\nabla F_{d,s}^{\star}(x)-\nabla F_{d,s}^{\star}(y)\right]\right\|^{q}\right]

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\begin{aligned} \overset{{\scriptscriptstyle\text{\eqref{70110a40-c2b6-4b8f-b790-e09561a73316}}}}{}&{=}\beta^{q}{\mathbb{E}}_{\xi\sim\mathbb{P}^{s}}\left[\left\|\left[\nabla f_{d}(\beta x,\xi)-\nabla f_{d}(\beta y,\xi)\right]-\left[\nabla F_{d,s}(\beta x)-\nabla F_{d,s}(\beta y)\right]\right\|^{q}\right]\\
\overset{{\scriptscriptstyle\text{\eqref{2130180a-a3a9-4040-8a04-214e99e99948}}}}{}&{=}\beta^{q}{\mathbb{E}}_{\xi\sim\mathbb{P}^{s}}\left[\left\|\left[L(\beta x-\bm{\xi})-L(\beta y-\bm{\xi})\right]-\left[L(\beta x-\theta_{s})-L(\beta y-\theta_{s})\right]\right\|^{q}\right]\\
&=0,\end{aligned}

hence
2.7
is satisfied. Moreover, taking
L=\frac{2\Delta}{r^{2}}
is enough to ensure that
F_{d,s}^{\star}
has
\Delta
–bounded sub-optimality (see (
C.1
)). On the other hand, for any
x,y\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
we have

\displaystyle\left\|\nabla F_{d,s}^{\star}(x)-\nabla F_{d,s}^{\star}(y)\right\|

\displaystyle{=}\beta\left\|\nabla F_{d,s}(\beta x)-\nabla F_{d,s}(\beta y)\right\|

\displaystyle=\beta^{2}L\left\|x-y\right\|,

(13)

and it’s enough to take
0<\beta\leq\sqrt{\frac{L_{1}}{L}}
to ensure the function
F_{d,s}^{\star}
has
L_{1}
–Lipschitz gradients. The rest of the proof (
steps 3
,
4
and
5
) is the same, using
L_{1}
instead of
\bar{L}
.

Remark C.3
.

As a last remark before proving
Lemma

C.1
, it is also worth noting that the stochastic function
f_{d}\colon\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}
used to prove the lower bound (
C.1
) still holds when one has access to high-order information (e.g., the Hessian), that is, under
2.1
,
2.2
,
2.3
and
2.5
, because by construction
f_{d}
is a quadratic function so, for any
s\in\left\{-1,1\right\}
and any
x,y\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
, we have

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}_{\xi\sim\mathbb{P}^{s}}\left[\left\|\nabla^{2}f^{\star}_{d}(x,\xi)-\nabla^{2}F^{\star}_{d,s}(x)\right\|_{\text{op}}^{q}\right]

\displaystyle=\beta^{2q}{\mathbb{E}}_{\xi\sim\mathbb{P}^{s}}\left[\left\|\nabla^{2}f_{d}(\beta x,\xi)-\nabla^{2}F_{d,s}(\beta x)\right\|_{\text{op}}^{q}\right]

\displaystyle=\beta^{2q}{\mathbb{E}}_{\xi\sim\mathbb{P}^{s}}\left[\left\|LI_{d}-LI_{d}\right\|_{\text{op}}^{q}\right]

\displaystyle=0,

where
I_{d}\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}
is the identity matrix. Hence,
2.5
is satisfied. Additionally, since
\nabla^{2}F^{\star}_{d,s}(x)=L\beta^{2}I_{d}
for any
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
then the Hessian of
F^{\star}_{d,s}
is
L_{2}
–Lipschitz continuous for any constant
L_{2}\geq 0
. Combining this observation with the arguments in the previous remark (see (
13
)), we obtain that
F^{\star}_{d,s}\in\mathcal{F}(\Delta,L_{1},L_{2})
for any
d\geq 1
and any
s\in\left\{-1,1\right\}
, as long as we choose

L=\frac{2\Delta}{r^{2}}\,\,\text{ and }\,\,0<\beta\leq\sqrt{\frac{L_{1}}{L}}.

The rest of the proof (
steps 3
,
4
and
5
) is the same.

Proof.

Let the accuracy parameter
\varepsilon>0
, initial sub-optimality
\Delta\geq 0
, the mean-squared smoothness parameter
\bar{L}
, and the variance parameter
\sigma_{1}\geq 0
be fixed, and
0<L\leq\bar{L}
to be specified late. Our proof follows the same step as in
6
except that, taking inspiration from
34
and the gradient oracle from
4
as recalled in
Definition

B.7
, we take two Bernoulli distributions instead of normal distributions.

Let us fix
d\geq 1
an integer and consider the following family of functions
f_{d}\colon\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\left\{-1,0,1\right\}\to\mathbb{R}
defined as

f_{d}(x,\xi):=\frac{L}{2}\left(\left\|x\right\|^{2}-2x_{1}\xi+r^{2}\right),

where
r\in\mathopen{(}0\mathclose{},1\mathclose{]}
is a fixed parameter (to be specified later),
(x,\xi)\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\left\{-1,0,1\right\}
and
x=(x_{1},\ldots,x_{d})
are its coordinates. Then, for
\beta>0
, we rescale the function
f_{d}
as

f_{d}^{\star}(x,\xi)\colon x\mapsto f_{d}(\beta x,\xi).

Next, we define the two probability distributions
\mathbb{P}^{1}=\mathrm{Ber}(r)
and
\mathbb{P}^{-1}=-\mathrm{Ber}(r)
(whose support is
\left\{-1,0\right\}
) and we let
\theta_{s}:=(rs,0,\ldots,0)\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
for
s\in\left\{-1,1\right\}
. Additionally, for any
s\in\left\{-1,1\right\}
, we define the function
F_{d,s}^{\star}\colon\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}
for all
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
as

F_{d,s}^{\star}(x):={\mathbb{E}}_{\xi\sim\mathbb{P}^{s}}\left[f_{d}^{\star}\left(x,\xi\right)\right]\overset{\scriptscriptstyle\text{(a)}}{=}\frac{L}{2}\left(\left\|\beta x\right\|^{2}-2\beta x_{1}rs+r^{2}\right)=\frac{L}{2}\left\|\beta x-\theta_{s}\right\|^{2}=:F_{d,s}(\beta x),

where (
) follows from the fact that, when
s=1
then
\xi\sim\mathrm{Ber}(r)
and
\mathbb{E}\left[\xi\right]=r=rs
, while when
s=-1
then
\xi\sim-\mathrm{Ber}(r)
and
\mathbb{E}\left[\xi\right]=-r=rs
.

Step 1:

Ensuring
F_{d,s}^{\star}
has
\Delta
–bounded initial sub-optimality (
2.1
)
.

To ensure
F_{d,s}^{\star}
satisfies
2.1
, let us compute the initial sub-optimality. Assuming the starting point is
x^{0}=0
, we have

F^{\star}_{d,s}(0)-\inf_{x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}F^{\star}_{d,s}(x)\overset{{\scriptscriptstyle\text{\eqref{70110a40-c2b6-4b8f-b790-e09561a73316}}}}{=}F_{d,s}(0)=\frac{Lr^{2}}{2},

thus, it’s enough to take
L=\frac{2\Delta}{r^{2}}<+\infty
(since
r>0)
so as to ensure the function
F_{d,s}^{\star}
has
\Delta
–bounded initial sub-optimality. In the next step, we will show that
F_{d,s}^{\star}
also has
\bar{L}
–Lipschitz gradients, as a consequence of the mean-squared smoothness property.

Step 2:

The Oracle Class and
2.3
and
2.6
.

Now, let us compute the
p
–th central moment, for any
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
and any
s\in\left\{-1,1\right\}
we have

\displaystyle{2}{\mathbb{E}}_{\xi\sim\mathbb{P}^{s}}\left[\left\|\nabla f_{d}^{\star}(x,\xi)-\nabla F_{d,s}^{\star}(x)\right\|^{p}\right]

\displaystyle\overset{{\scriptscriptstyle\text{\eqref{70110a40-c2b6-4b8f-b790-e09561a73316}}}}{=}

\displaystyle\beta^{p}\,{\mathbb{E}}_{\xi\sim\mathbb{P}^{s}}\left[\left\|\nabla f_{d}(\beta x,\xi)-\nabla F_{d,s}(\beta x)\right\|^{p}\right]

(17)

\displaystyle\overset{\scriptscriptstyle\text{(a)}}{=}

\displaystyle\beta^{p}\,{\mathbb{E}}_{\xi\sim\mathbb{P}^{s}}\left[\left\|L\left(\beta x-\bm{\xi}\right)-L\left(\beta x-\theta_{s}\right)\right\|^{p}\right]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left(L\beta\right)^{p}{\mathbb{E}}_{\xi\sim\mathbb{P}^{s}}\left[\left\lvert\xi-rs\right\rvert^{p}\right]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left(L\beta r\right)^{p}{\mathbb{E}}_{\xi\sim\mathbb{P}^{s}}\left[\left\lvert\frac{\xi}{r}-s\right\rvert^{p}\right]

\displaystyle\overset{\scriptscriptstyle\text{(b)}}{=}

\displaystyle\left(L\beta r\right)^{p}\left((1-r)\left\lvert s\right\rvert^{p}+r\left\lvert\frac{s}{r}-s\right\rvert^{p}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left(L\beta r\right)^{p}\left((1-r)+r\left\lvert\frac{1}{r}-1\right\rvert^{p}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left(L\beta r\right)^{p}(1-r)\left(1+\left(\frac{1-r}{r}\right)^{p-1}\right)

\displaystyle\overset{\scriptscriptstyle\text{(c)}}{=}

\displaystyle\left(L\beta\right)^{p}r(1-r)\left(r^{p-1}+\left(1-r\right)^{p-1}\right)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle 2\left(L\beta\right)^{p}r(1-r),

where in (
) we let
\bm{\xi}=(\xi,0,\ldots,0)\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
and the gradient of
f_{d}
and
F_{d,s}
are given, for any
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
by

\nabla f_{d}(x,\xi)=L(x-\bm{\xi})\,\,\text{ and }\,\,\nabla F_{d,s}(x)=L(x-\theta_{s}),

notably,
{\mathbb{E}}_{\xi\sim\mathbb{P}^{s}}\left[\nabla f_{d}^{\star}(x,\xi)\right]=\nabla F_{d,s}^{\star}(x)
. In (
) we use the fact that
\left\lvert s\right\rvert=1
while in (
) we use both
p>1
and
r\in\mathopen{(}0\mathclose{},1\mathclose{]}
to bound
r^{p-1}+\left(1-r\right)^{p-1}
by
2
. Hence, following (
17
) we need to guarantee

2\left(L\beta\right)^{p}r(1-r)\leq\sigma_{1}^{p}\,\,\text{ so }\,\,2^{\frac{1}{p}}L\beta\left(r(1-r)\right)^{\frac{1}{p}}\leq\sigma_{1}.

Now, computing the mean-squared smoothness constant, we have, for
q\in\mathopen{(}1\mathclose{},2\mathclose{]}
,
s\in\left\{-1,1\right\}
and any
x,y\in\mathbb{R}^{d}

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}_{\xi\sim\mathbb{P}^{s}}\left[\left\|\nabla f_{d}^{\star}(x,\xi)-\nabla f_{d}^{\star}(y,\xi)\right\|^{q}\right]

\displaystyle{=}\beta^{q}\,{\mathbb{E}}_{\xi\sim\mathbb{P}^{s}}\left[\left\|\nabla f_{d}(\beta x,\xi)-\nabla f_{d}(\beta y,\xi)\right\|^{q}\right]

\displaystyle{=}\left(L\beta\right)^{q}\,{\mathbb{E}}_{\xi\sim\mathbb{P}^{s}}\left[\left\|\beta x-\beta y\right\|^{q}\right]

\displaystyle=\left(L\beta^{2}\right)^{q}\left\|x-y\right\|^{q},

and it suffices to ensure

\left(L\beta^{2}\right)^{q}\leq\bar{L}^{q},\,\,\text{ that is, }\,\,L\beta^{2}\leq\bar{L},

so as to satisfy
2.6
.

Step 3:

Choice of
\beta
,
r
.

It remains to choose
\beta>0
,
r\in\mathopen{(}0\mathclose{},1\mathclose{]}
(universal constant) to satisfy the two inequalities (
C.1
) and (
C.1
). Additionally, let
c^{\prime}>0
, to be fixed later, be an universal constant such that
0<\varepsilon\leq c^{\prime}\sqrt{\bar{L}\Delta}
. From inequality (
C.1
) we have

\beta\leq\sqrt{\frac{\bar{L}}{L}}=\frac{1}{L}\sqrt{\bar{L}L}=\frac{\sqrt{2\bar{L}\Delta}}{Lr},

hence for any constant
c^{\prime}>0
, taking
\beta=\frac{\varepsilon\sqrt{2}}{c^{\prime}Lr}>0
gives

\beta=\frac{\varepsilon\sqrt{2}}{c^{\prime}Lr}\leq\frac{c^{\prime}\sqrt{\bar{L}\Delta}\sqrt{2}}{c^{\prime}Lr}=\frac{\sqrt{2\bar{L}\Delta}}{Lr},

as desired. Plugging back this value in the inequality (
C.1
) we need to have

\frac{2^{\frac{1}{p}}\sqrt{2}}{c^{\prime}}\varepsilon r^{\frac{1}{p}-1}(1-r)^{\frac{1}{p}}\leq\sigma_{1},\,\,\text{ i.e., }\,\,\frac{2^{\frac{1}{p}}\sqrt{2}}{c^{\prime}}\left(\frac{\varepsilon}{\sigma_{1}}\right)(1-r)^{\frac{1}{p}}\leq r^{\frac{p-1}{p}},

which is equivalent to

C^{\frac{p}{p-1}}\left(\frac{\varepsilon}{\sigma_{1}}\right)^{\frac{p}{p-1}}(1-r)^{\frac{1}{p-1}}\leq r,

where we set
C:=\frac{2^{\frac{1}{p}}\sqrt{2}}{c^{\prime}}
. Thus, as
1-r\leq 1
, it is enough to take

r=\min\left\{1,\left(\frac{C\varepsilon}{\sigma_{1}}\right)^{\frac{p}{p-1}}\right\}>0,

and now all the inequalities are satisfied so do
2.1
,
2.3
and
2.6
hold.

Step 4:

Transforming the Optimization Problem into a Function Identification Problem
.

We now continue to follow the proof of
4
. First, let us randomized the selection of the instances
\{F_{d,s}^{\star}\}_{s\in\left\{-1,1\right\}}
by drawing
s
uniformly in the set
\left\{-1,1\right\}
. Hence, we let
S\sim\mathcal{U}\left(\left\{-1,1\right\}\right)
and consider any algorithm
A
that takes as input the iid samples
\xi_{1},\ldots,\xi_{T}\sim\mathbb{P}^{S}
, where
T
is the number of queries, and which returns the (random) iterate
\widehat{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
. We now bound the expected norm of the gradient at
\widehat{x}
. To do so, let us define the (random) quantity
\widehat{S}:=\mathop{\mathrm{arg\,min}}_{s\in\left\{-1,1\right\}}\|\nabla F^{\star}_{d,s}\left(\widehat{x}\right)\!\|
, breaking ties arbitrarily. Then if
S\neq\widehat{S}
we have, by definition of
\widehat{S}
,

\left\|\nabla F^{\star}_{d,S}\left(\widehat{x}\right)\right\|\geq\left\|\nabla F^{\star}_{d,\widehat{S}}\left(\widehat{x}\right)\right\|,

hence

\displaystyle 2\mathbb{E}\left[\left\|\nabla F^{\star}_{d,S}\left(\widehat{x}\right)\right\|\right]

\displaystyle\geq\mathbb{E}\left[\left\|\nabla F^{\star}_{d,S}\left(\widehat{x}\right)\right\|\right]+\mathbb{E}\left[\left\|\nabla F^{\star}_{d,\widehat{S}}\left(\widehat{x}\right)\right\|\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left[\left\|\nabla F^{\star}_{d,S}\left(\widehat{x}\right)\right\|+\left\|\nabla F^{\star}_{d,\widehat{S}}\left(\widehat{x}\right)\right\|\right]

\displaystyle{\geq}\inf_{x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left(\left\|\nabla F^{\star}_{d,1}(x)\right\|+\left\|\nabla F^{\star}_{d,-1}(x)\right\|\right)

\displaystyle{=}L\beta\inf_{x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left(\left\|\beta x-\theta_{1}\right\|+\left\|\beta x-\theta_{-1}\right\|\right)

\displaystyle{\geq}L\beta\left\|\theta_{1}-\theta_{-1}\right\|

\displaystyle=2L\beta r,

(21)

where in (
) we use
S\neq\widehat{S}
and that both belongs to
\left\{-1,1\right\}
. In (
) we use the triangle inequality. Hence,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\left\|\nabla F^{\star}_{d,S}\left(\widehat{x}\right)\right\|\right]

\displaystyle{\geq}L\beta r\mathbb{P}\left(\left\|\nabla F^{\star}_{d,S}\left(\widehat{x}\right)\right\|\geq L\beta r\right)

\displaystyle{\geq}L\beta r\mathbb{P}\left(\widehat{S}\neq S\right),

since
L\beta r>0
and the last inequality follows from the lower bound in (
21
), implied by the event
\{\widehat{S}\neq S\}
.

Step 5:

Lower Bounding the Misidentification Probability
\mathbb{P}\left(\widehat{S}\neq S\right)
.

Next, for
s\in\left\{-1,1\right\}
let
\mathbb{P}_{T}^{s}=s\mathrm{Ber}^{\otimes T}(r)
be the law of
\left(\xi_{1},\ldots,\xi_{T}\right)
conditioned on the event
\left\{S=s\right\}
, then

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left(\widehat{S}\neq S\right)

\displaystyle=1-\mathbb{P}\left(\widehat{S}=S\right)

\displaystyle=1-\left(\mathbb{P}\left(S=1\right)\mathbb{P}\left(\widehat{S}=S\middle|S=1\right)+\mathbb{P}\left(S=-1\right)\mathbb{P}\left(\widehat{S}=S\middle|S=-1\right)\right)

\displaystyle=1-\frac{1}{2}\left(\mathbb{P}_{T}^{1}\left(\widehat{S}=1\right)+\mathbb{P}_{T}^{-1}\left(\widehat{S}\neq 1\right)\right)

\displaystyle\leq 1-\frac{1}{2}\sup_{A\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{T}\textnormal{ mesurable}}\left(\mathbb{P}^{1}_{T}(A)+\mathbb{P}^{-1}_{T}(A^{\texttt{c}})\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}-\frac{1}{2}\sup_{A\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{T}\textnormal{ mesurable}}\left(\mathbb{P}^{1}_{T}(A)-\mathbb{P}^{-1}_{T}(A)\right)

\displaystyle{=}\frac{1}{2}\left(1-\left\|\mathbb{P}_{T}^{1}-\mathbb{P}_{T}^{-1}\right\|_{\textnormal{TV}}\right),

where (
) follows form the definition of the total variation distance. Then we compute
\left\|\mathbb{P}_{T}^{1}-\mathbb{P}_{T}^{-1}\right\|_{\textnormal{TV}}
, to do so observe that the support of
\mathbb{P}_{T}^{1}
is
\left\{0,1\right\}^{T}
and the support of
\mathbb{P}_{T}^{-1}
is
\left\{-1,0\right\}^{T}
thus, if we let
\mathbf{0}:=(0,\ldots,0)
,

\displaystyle\left\|\mathbb{P}_{T}^{1}-\mathbb{P}_{T}^{-1}\right\|_{\textnormal{TV}}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{x\in\left\{-1,0,1\right\}^{T}}\left\lvert\mathbb{P}_{T}^{1}(x)-\mathbb{P}_{T}^{-1}(x)\right\rvert

\displaystyle{=}\frac{1}{2}\sum_{x\in\left\{-1,0\right\}^{T}\setminus\left\{\mathbf{0}\right\}}\mathbb{P}_{T}^{-1}(x)+\frac{1}{2}\sum_{x\in\left\{0,1\right\}^{T}\setminus\left\{\mathbf{0}\right\}}\mathbb{P}_{T}^{1}(x)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\left(1-(1-r)^{T}\right)+\frac{1}{2}\left(1-(1-r)^{T}\right)

\displaystyle=1-(1-r)^{T},

where (
) follows from
\mathbb{P}_{T}^{1}\left(\mathbf{0}\right)=(1-r)^{T}=\mathbb{P}_{T}^{-1}\left(\mathbf{0}\right)
along with the fact that
\left\{-1,0\right\}^{T}\cap\left\{0,1\right\}^{T}=\left\{\mathbf{0}\right\}
. Hence, we obtain

\mathbb{P}\left(\widehat{S}\neq S\right)\geq\frac{1}{2}(1-r)^{T},

and plugging this bound into (
21
) gives

\mathbb{E}\left[\left\|\nabla F^{\star}_{d,S}\left(\widehat{x}\right)\right\|\right]\geq\frac{L\beta r}{2}(1-r)^{T}.

Also, note that

\displaystyle\max\left\{\mathbb{E}\left[\left\|\nabla F^{\star}_{d,1}\left(\widehat{x}\right)\right\|\right],\mathbb{E}\left[\left\|\nabla F^{\star}_{d,-1}\left(\widehat{x}\right)\right\|\right]\right\}

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\left(\mathbb{E}\left[\left\|\nabla F^{\star}_{d,1}\left(\widehat{x}\right)\right\|\right]+\mathbb{E}\left[\left\|\nabla F^{\star}_{d,-1}\left(\widehat{x}\right)\right\|\right]\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\left\|\nabla F^{\star}_{d,S}\left(\widehat{x}\right)\right\|\right].

It remains to lower bound adequately the quantity
\frac{L\beta r}{2}(1-r)^{T}
. For this, we consider two cases:

•

if
\sigma_{1}>C\varepsilon
then
1>\frac{C\varepsilon}{\sigma_{1}}
so
1>\left(\frac{C\varepsilon}{\sigma_{1}}\right)^{\frac{p}{p-1}}
thus
r=\left(\frac{C\varepsilon}{\sigma_{1}}\right)^{\frac{p}{p-1}}
so

\displaystyle\frac{L\beta r}{2}(1-r)^{T}

\displaystyle=\frac{\varepsilon\sqrt{2}}{2c^{\prime}}(1-r)^{T},

and, assume we have
T\leq\frac{1}{2r}
then

(1-r)^{T}\overset{\scriptscriptstyle\text{(a)}}{\geq}1-rT\geq\frac{1}{2},

where (
) follows from the Bernoulli’s inequality (see
Lemma

H.12
) thus

\max\left\{\mathbb{E}\left[\left\|\nabla F^{\star}_{d,1}\left(\widehat{x}\right)\right\|\right],\mathbb{E}\left[\left\|\nabla F^{\star}_{d,-1}\left(\widehat{x}\right)\right\|\right]\right\}\geq\frac{\varepsilon\sqrt{2}}{4c^{\prime}},

and it’s enough to take
c^{\prime}=\frac{1}{8}
to ensure

\max\left\{\mathbb{E}\left[\left\|\nabla F^{\star}_{d,1}\left(\widehat{x}\right)\right\|\right],\mathbb{E}\left[\left\|\nabla F^{\star}_{d,-1}\left(\widehat{x}\right)\right\|\right]\right\}\geq 2\varepsilon\sqrt{2}>2\varepsilon

So, when
T\leq\frac{1}{2r}
it is not possible to reach an
\varepsilon
–stationary point on both
F^{\star}_{d,1}
and on
F^{\star}_{d,-1}
.

Hence, we deduce that we must have

T>\frac{1}{2r}=\frac{1}{2}\left(\frac{\sigma_{1}}{C\varepsilon}\right)^{\frac{p}{p-1}}=\Omega(1)\cdot\left(\frac{\sigma_{1}}{\varepsilon}\right)^{\frac{p}{p-1}}\geq\Omega(1),

as desired,

•

if
\sigma_{1}\leq C\varepsilon
then
r=1
and

\left(\frac{\sigma_{1}}{C\varepsilon}\right)^{\frac{p}{p-1}}\leq 1,

and since for any
s\in\left\{-1,1\right\}
we have

\left\|\nabla F^{\star}_{d,s}(0)\right\|\overset{{\scriptscriptstyle\text{\eqref{2130180a-a3a9-4040-8a04-214e99e99948}}}}{=}L\beta r=\frac{\varepsilon\sqrt{2}}{c^{\prime}}=8\varepsilon\sqrt{2}>2\varepsilon,

so at least one query is required to reach an
\varepsilon
–stationary point, i.e.,
T\geq 1
from where

T\geq 1\geq\left(\frac{\sigma_{1}}{C\varepsilon}\right)^{\frac{p}{p-1}}=\Omega(1)\cdot\left(\frac{\sigma_{1}}{\varepsilon}\right)^{\frac{p}{p-1}},

which achieves the proof of the lemma.

∎

Remark C.4
.

Let us expand on the proof strategy of the above lemma. First, we start by defining a stochastic function
f\colon(x,\xi)\mapsto\frac{L}{2}\left(\left\|x\right\|^{2}-2x_{1}\xi+r^{2}\right)
and we then provide two probability distributions
\mathbb{P}^{-}
and
\mathbb{P}^{+}
for the random variable
\xi
which allows to define two deterministic functions

F_{-}\colon x\mapsto{\mathbb{E}}_{\xi\sim\mathbb{P}_{-}}\left[f(x,\xi)\right]\,\,\text{ and }\,\,F_{+}\colon x\mapsto{\mathbb{E}}_{\xi\sim\mathbb{P}_{+}}\left[f(x,\xi)\right].

The strategy to establish a lower bound then is to randomized the initial choice of the function by choosing
F_{-}
or
F_{+}
with probability
\frac{1}{2}
and lower bound the optimization error on the norm of the gradient by the misidentification error, in other word, how many samples
\xi^{(1)},\ldots,\xi^{(T)}
are needed in expectation to distinguish between the two distributions
\mathbb{P}^{-}
and
\mathbb{P}^{+}
.

Contrary to
4
